docs/paper/related_work.tex:10:\citet{ferrando2025execute} compared regular, compact, and dynamic scratchpad strategies, finding that compact traces (recording only changed variables) perform comparably to full traces---directly supporting our finding that complete value information is redundant.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:23:TraceCoder \citep{huang2026tracecoder} inserts diagnostic probes at selected locations rather than recording full traces, improving Pass@1 by 34.4\% for LLM-generated code debugging.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:12:To test whether exception-based trace compression transfers across model families, we compare Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct under four input conditions: \texttt{exception\_only} (Tier~1 exception events), VOTA (exception + assertion-point + loop compression), \texttt{full\_trace} (complete execution trace), and \texttt{no\_trace} (source code only).
docs/paper/experiments.tex:19:\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Qwen-2.5-7B} & \textbf{DeepSeek-6.7B} & \textbf{Gap} \\
docs/paper/experiments.tex:24:full\_trace & 81.76\% & N/A$^\ddag$ & --- \\
docs/paper/experiments.tex:30:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/experiments.tex:35:Under \texttt{exception\_only}, Qwen achieves 99.83\% $\pm$ 0.00 across three seeds, while DeepSeek shows a bimodal pattern: seeds 42 and 456 both achieve 99.33\% (gap = 0.50pp), while seed 123 yields 91.09\% (gap = 8.74pp), attributable to suboptimal LoRA initialization (fail recall = 70.5\% vs.\ 100\% for other seeds).
docs/paper/experiments.tex:37:Under VOTA, the gap widens to 14.96pp: DeepSeek drops to 84.45\% while Qwen remains at 99.41\%.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:38:Post-hoc tokenizer analysis reveals a confound: measured on the training set using each model's tokenizer, the DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}759 tokens per VOTA sample versus 1{,}908 for Qwen, causing 91.7\% of DeepSeek VOTA training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:39:Unlike the DeepSeek \texttt{full\_trace} condition (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}, $^\ddag$), where right-truncation removed the verdict label entirely, the VOTA training framework preserves the verdict label but truncates the trace content (input), so the model trains with incomplete diagnostic information for the vast majority of the dataset.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:41:However, a post-hoc group analysis provides directional evidence: partitioning DeepSeek VOTA test samples by whether their training-set counterparts exceeded the context window, intact samples ($N{=}99$) achieve 100\% accuracy while truncated samples ($N{=}1{,}091$) achieve 83.04\% (95\% CI [80.75\%, 85.24\%]; Fisher exact $p{<}0.001$), with all 185 errors concentrated in the fail class of the truncated group (fail recall $=$ 31.23\%).
docs/paper/experiments.tex:43:Per-class analysis (Appendix~\ref{sec:per_class}) reveals the mechanism: DeepSeek VOTA achieves 100\% fail precision but only 45.43\% fail recall, systematically defaulting to \texttt{pass}---in contrast to Qwen VOTA, which achieves 100\% fail recall.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:45:The \texttt{no\_trace} baseline confirms that source code alone provides limited signal (75.13\% for Qwen, 73.36\% for DeepSeek), only marginally above majority-class guessing (71.51\%).
docs/paper/experiments.tex:49:DeepSeek shows a bimodal pattern ($n{=}3$): seeds 42 and 456 both achieve 99.33\% (8 errors each, fail recall = 100\%), while seed 123 yields 91.09\% (fail recall = 70.5\%), consistent with LoRA initialization sensitivity on the smaller model.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:109:This two-factor decomposition is consistent across models: the VOTA cross-model gap (14.96pp, Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}) is confounded by training-time trace truncation on DeepSeek (\S\ref{sec:cross_model}), which amplifies information overload by removing diagnostic trace content from the majority of training samples.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:153:\caption{Cross-model accuracy gap (Qwen $-$ DeepSeek) across trace conditions. Exception-based signals transfer across models (0.50pp gap), while VOTA is model-dependent (14.96pp gap).}
docs/paper/main.tex:20:We present the first systematic component ablation of trace representations for \emph{verdict prediction}---classifying whether a test passes or fails---across two models (Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B).
docs/paper/main.tex:21:Our key finding is that exception events alone achieve near-perfect accuracy on Qwen (99.83\% $\pm$ 0.00, 3 seeds) and are the most effective component on DeepSeek (2/3 seeds at 99.33\%; 1 outlier at 91.09\% from LoRA initialization sensitivity), while a multi-component representation (VOTA) degrades by 14.96pp on the weaker model, confounded by training-time trace truncation.
docs/paper/main.tex:22:We decompose the gap between full traces and compressed representations into gradient dilution (26\%) and information overload (74\%), establishing that irrelevant trace content---not training signal quality---is the primary bottleneck.
docs/paper/conclusion.tex:7:Exception events alone achieve 99.83\% $\pm$ 0.00 on Qwen (3 seeds) and 99.33\% on 2/3 DeepSeek seeds, with one outlier at 91.09\% from LoRA initialization sensitivity.
docs/paper/conclusion.tex:9:In contrast, the full VOTA representation degrades by 14.96pp on DeepSeek---a gap confounded by training-time trace truncation (the majority of DeepSeek VOTA samples exceed the context window; \S\ref{sec:cross_model}) and model capability differences, which cannot be disentangled in the current design.
docs/paper/conclusion.tex:12:The 17.65pp gap between full traces and VOTA decomposes into gradient dilution (26\%, from training on irrelevant trace tokens) and information overload (74\%, from processing irrelevant trace lines at inference).
docs/paper/main.log:740:\OT1/ptm/m/n/10.95 com-pare Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct and DeepSeek-
docs/paper/main.log:887:\OT1/ptm/m/n/10.95 DeepSeek VOTA ac-cu-racy mono-ton-i-cally in-
docs/paper/discussion.tex:12:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/discussion.tex:18:Two of three DeepSeek seeds match Qwen-level accuracy (99.33\%); the outlier (seed 123, 91.09\%) is attributable to LoRA initialization landing in a suboptimal basin (fail recall = 70.5\%), not to a failure of the exception signal itself---even this outlier outperforms VOTA (84.45\%) by 6.64pp.
docs/paper/discussion.tex:19:The VOTA cross-model gap is confounded by two factors: (1)~tokenizer efficiency---the DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}759 tokens per VOTA sample versus Qwen's 1{,}908, causing 91.7\% of training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window, truncating trace content while preserving the verdict label---and (2)~base model capability differences, consistent with findings that SFT generalization depends on pretrain quality \citep{yang2024generalization}.
docs/paper/discussion.tex:26:The DeepSeek \texttt{exception\_only} results reveal two instructive training dynamics.
docs/paper/discussion.tex:32:This demonstrates that token-level training loss can mask fundamental differences in sample-level classification ability, suggesting a parallel to the eval-loss/accuracy decoupling observed in DeepSeek VOTA (where eval loss rose 65\% from epoch 1 to 3 while accuracy improved by 6.3pp) at the training phase.
docs/paper/discussion.tex:35:The contrast with VOTA is instructive: DeepSeek VOTA accuracy monotonically increases from 78.15\% to 84.45\% across epochs, but is consistent with a performance limit associated with the high trace truncation rate (\S\ref{sec:cross_model})---the model progressively extracts signal from samples with intact trace content but cannot fully compensate for the information loss in the truncated majority.
docs/paper/discussion.tex:56:Third, DeepSeek \texttt{exception\_only} exhibits seed-dependent variance: 2/3 seeds achieve 99.33\% while seed 123 yields 91.09\% ($\sigma{=}4.76$pp, $n{=}3$), attributable to LoRA initialization sensitivity (fail recall = 70.5\% for the outlier vs.\ 100\% for others).
docs/paper/discussion.tex:58:Whether training stabilization techniques (e.g., larger warmup ratio, LoRA rank adjustment) can eliminate DeepSeek's seed sensitivity is unexplored.
docs/paper/discussion.tex:60:Fourth, the DeepSeek \texttt{full\_trace} condition was invalidated because the DeepSeek tokenizer produced sequences exceeding 2,048 tokens, causing the verdict label to be truncated during training; the model consequently failed to learn the classification task, and we report it as N/A rather than presenting potentially misleading results.
docs/paper/method.tex:68:\item \textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \citep{deepseek2024coder}: Pretrained on 2T tokens. Used for cross-model validation to test whether findings generalize beyond a single model family.
docs/paper/method.tex:84:This condition uses the same full trace as input but computes loss only on the verdict token, masking all trace tokens during training (loss weight = 0 on trace positions).
docs/paper/appendix.tex:8:\caption{Per-class precision, recall, and F1 for all experimental conditions (seed 42). DeepSeek VOTA achieves 100\% fail precision but only 45.43\% fail recall, indicating systematic bias toward predicting \texttt{pass}. DeepSeek \texttt{exception\_only} seed 42 achieves 100\% fail recall, but seed 123 drops to 70.5\% (see Table~\ref{tab:full_deepseek}).}
docs/paper/appendix.tex:24:No Trace          & DeepSeek-6.7B & 73.36 & 81.71 & 80.85 & 81.28 & 53.16 & 54.57 & 53.86 \\
docs/paper/appendix.tex:25:VOTA              & DeepSeek-6.7B & 84.45 & 82.14 & 100.00 & 90.20 & 100.00 & 45.43 & 62.47 \\
docs/paper/appendix.tex:26:Exception Only    & DeepSeek-6.7B & 99.33 & 100.00 & 99.06 & 99.53 & 97.69 & 100.00 & 98.83 \\
docs/paper/appendix.tex:186:\caption{Full results for DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B across all conditions and seeds. \texttt{exception\_only} shows bimodal seed behavior: seeds 42 and 456 achieve 99.33\%, while seed 123 yields 91.09\% (LoRA initialization outlier, fail recall = 70.5\%).}
docs/paper/appendix.tex:199:\texttt{full\_trace}     & 42  & N/A   & label truncated (context overflow) \\
docs/paper/introduction.tex:13:Using 591 problems from HumanEval \citep{chen2021evaluating} and MBPP-sanitized \citep{austin2021program} with model-generated solutions and execution traces, we decompose a multi-component trace representation (VOTA: exception events + assertion-point comparisons + loop compression) into individual components and evaluate each in isolation across two models: Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct.
docs/paper/introduction.tex:15:Our results reveal a striking finding: \textbf{exception events alone achieve near-perfect verdict prediction on Qwen (99.83\% $\pm$ 0.00, 3 seeds) and are the most effective component on DeepSeek, with 2/3 seeds matching Qwen-level accuracy (99.33\%); the remaining seed (91.09\%) reflects LoRA initialization sensitivity rather than a signal limitation.}
docs/paper/introduction.tex:16:Even the DeepSeek outlier seed (91.09\%) outperforms VOTA (84.45\%) and no-trace (73.36\%) by wide margins, confirming exception events as the dominant trace component across models.
docs/paper/introduction.tex:17:In contrast, the full VOTA representation---which combines exception events with assertion-point and loop information---degrades by 14.96pp on DeepSeek.
docs/paper/introduction.tex:18:Post-hoc analysis reveals the mechanism: the DeepSeek tokenizer produces longer sequences than Qwen for identical inputs, causing 91.7\% of VOTA training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window; the training framework preserves the verdict label but truncates the trace content, so the model trains on incomplete diagnostic information.
docs/paper/introduction.tex:21:We further decompose the gap between full trace and VOTA into two mechanisms---\emph{gradient dilution} (26\% of the gap) and \emph{information overload} (74\%)---showing that the primary bottleneck is not training signal quality but the presence of irrelevant trace content.
docs/paper/introduction.tex:24:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/introduction.tex:28:\item \textbf{Exception signal transfer}: We show that exception events alone achieve 99.83\% $\pm$ 0.00 on Qwen (3 seeds) and 99.33\% on 2/3 DeepSeek seeds, with one outlier at 91.09\% from LoRA initialization sensitivity; even the outlier exceeds VOTA (84.45\%) by 6.64pp (\S\ref{sec:cross_model}).
docs/paper/introduction.tex:29:\item \textbf{Information density decomposition}: We decompose the full-trace-to-VOTA gap into gradient dilution (26\%) and information overload (74\%), identifying the primary bottleneck for trace-based prediction (\S\ref{sec:density}).

others).\ndocs/paper/discussion.tex:58:Whether training stabilization techniques (e.g., larger warmup ratio, LoRA rank adjustment) can eliminate DeepSeek's seed sensitivity is unexplored.\ndocs/paper/discussion.tex:60:Fourth, the DeepSeek \\texttt{full\\_trace} condition was invalidated because the DeepSeek tokenizer produced sequences exceeding 2,048 tokens, causing the verdict label to be truncated during training; the model consequently failed to learn the classification task, and we report it as N/A rather than presenting potentially misleading results.\ndocs/paper/method.tex:68:\\item \\textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \\citep{deepseek2024coder}: Pretrained on 2T tokens. Used for cross-model validation to test whether findings generalize beyond a single model family.\ndocs/paper/method.tex:84:This condition uses the same full trace as input but computes loss only on the verdict token, masking all trace tokens during training (loss weight = 0 on trace positions).\ndocs/paper/appendix.tex:8:\\caption{Per-class precision, recall, and F1 for all experimental conditions (seed 42). DeepSeek VOTA achieves 100\\% fail precision but only 45.43\\% fail recall, indicating systematic bias toward predicting \\texttt{pass}. DeepSeek \\texttt{exception\\_only} seed 42 achieves 100\\% fail recall, but seed 123 drops to 70.5\\% (see Table~\\ref{tab:full_deepseek}).}\ndocs/paper/appendix.tex:24:No Trace          & DeepSeek-6.7B & 73.36 & 81.71 & 80.85 & 81.28 & 53.16 & 54.57 & 53.86 \\\\\ndocs/paper/appendix.tex:25:VOTA              & DeepSeek-6.7B & 84.45 & 82.14 & 100.00 & 90.20 & 100.00 & 45.43 & 62.47 \\\\\ndocs/paper/appendix.tex:26:Exception Only    & DeepSeek-6.7B & 99.33 & 100.00 & 99.06 & 99.53 & 97.69 & 100.00 & 98.83 \\\\\ndocs/paper/appendix.tex:186:\\caption{Full results for DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B across all conditions and seeds. \\texttt{exception\\_only} shows bimodal seed behavior: seeds 42 and 456 achieve 99.33\\%, while seed 123 yields 91.09\\% (LoRA initialization outlier, fail recall = 70.5\\%).}\ndocs/paper/appendix.tex:199:\\texttt{full\\_trace}     & 42  & N/A   & label truncated (context overflow) \\\\\ndocs/paper/introduction.tex:13:Using 591 problems from HumanEval \\citep{chen2021evaluating} and MBPP-sanitized \\citep{austin2021program} with model-generated solutions and execution traces, we decompose a multi-component trace representation (VOTA: exception events + assertion-point comparisons + loop compression) into individual components and evaluate each in isolation across two models: Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct.\ndocs/paper/introduction.tex:15:Our results reveal a striking finding: \\textbf{exception events alone achieve near-perfect verdict prediction on Qwen (99.83\\% $\\pm$ 0.00, 3 seeds) and are the most effective component on DeepSeek, with 2/3 seeds matching Qwen-level accuracy (99.33\\%); the remaining seed (91.09\\%) reflects LoRA initialization sensitivity rather than a signal limitation.}\ndocs/paper/introduction.tex:16:Even the DeepSeek outlier seed (91.09\\%) outperforms VOTA (84.45\\%) and no-trace (73.36\\%) by wide margins, confirming exception events as the dominant trace component across models.\ndocs/paper/introduction.tex:17:In contrast, the full VOTA representation---which combines exception events with assertion-point and loop information---degrades by 14.96pp on DeepSeek.\ndocs/paper/introduction.tex:18:Post-hoc analysis reveals the mechanism: the DeepSeek tokenizer produces longer sequences than Qwen for identical inputs, causing 91.7\\% of VOTA training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window; the training framework preserves the verdict label but truncates the trace content, so the model trains on incomplete diagnostic information.\ndocs/paper/introduction.tex:21:We further decompose the gap between full trace and VOTA into two mechanisms---\\emph{gradient dilution} (26\\% of the gap) and \\emph{information overload} (74\\%)---showing that the primary bottleneck is not training signal quality but the presence of irrelevant trace content.\ndocs/paper/introduction.tex:24:[Omitted long matching line]\ndocs/paper/introduction.tex:28:\\item \\textbf{Exception signal transfer}: We show that exception events alone achieve 99.83\\% $\\pm$ 0.00 on Qwen (3 seeds) and 99.33\\% on 2/3 DeepSeek seeds, with one outlier at 91.09\\% from LoRA initialization sensitivity; even the outlier exceeds VOTA (84.45\\%) by 6.64pp (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}).\ndocs/paper/introduction.tex:29:\\item \\textbf{Information density decomposition}: We decompose the full-trace-to-VOTA gap into gradient dilution (26\\%) and information overload (74\\%), identifying the primary bottleneck for trace-based prediction (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).\ndocs/paper/references.bib:59:  title={DeepSeek-Coder: When the Large Language Model Meets Programming -- The Rise of Code Intelligence},\ndocs/paper/main.aux:339:[Omitted long matching line]\ndocs/paper/main.aux:340:[Omitted long matching line]\ndocs/paper/main.aux:520:\\@writefile{lof}{\\contentsline {figure}{\\numberline {3}{\\ignorespaces Cross-model accuracy gap (Qwen $-$ DeepSeek) across trace conditions. 12:To test whether exception-based trace compression transfers across model families, we compare Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct under four input conditions: \texttt{exception\_only} (Tier~1 exception events), VOTA (exception + assertion-point + loop compression), \texttt{full\_trace} (complete execution trace), and \texttt{no\_trace} (source code only).
19:\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Qwen-2.5-7B} & \textbf{DeepSeek-6.7B} & \textbf{Gap} \\
24:full\_trace & 81.76\% & N/A$^\ddag$ & --- \\
30:[Omitted long matching line]
35:Under \texttt{exception\_only}, Qwen achieves 99.83\% $\pm$ 0.00 across three seeds, while DeepSeek shows a bimodal pattern: seeds 42 and 456 both achieve 99.33\% (gap = 0.50pp), while seed 123 yields 91.09\% (gap = 8.74pp), attributable to suboptimal LoRA initialization (fail recall = 70.5\% vs.\ 100\% for other seeds).
37:Under VOTA, the gap widens to 14.96pp: DeepSeek drops to 84.45\% while Qwen remains at 99.41\%.
38:Post-hoc tokenizer analysis reveals a confound: measured on the training set using each model's tokenizer, the DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}759 tokens per VOTA sample versus 1{,}908 for Qwen, causing 91.7\% of DeepSeek VOTA training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window.
39:Unlike the DeepSeek \texttt{full\_trace} condition (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}, $^\ddag$), where right-truncation removed the verdict label entirely, the VOTA training framework preserves the verdict label but truncates the trace content (input), so the model trains with incomplete diagnostic information for the vast majority of the dataset.
41:However, a post-hoc group analysis provides directional evidence: partitioning DeepSeek VOTA test samples by whether their training-set counterparts exceeded the context window, intact samples ($N{=}99$) achieve 100\% accuracy while truncated samples ($N{=}1{,}091$) achieve 83.04\% (95\% CI [80.75\%, 85.24\%]; Fisher exact $p{<}0.001$), with all 185 errors concentrated in the fail class of the truncated group (fail recall $=$ 31.23\%).
43:Per-class analysis (Appendix~\ref{sec:per_class}) reveals the mechanism: DeepSeek VOTA achieves 100\% fail precision but only 45.43\% fail recall, systematically defaulting to \texttt{pass}---in contrast to Qwen VOTA, which achieves 100\% fail recall.
45:The \texttt{no\_trace} baseline confirms that source code alone provides limited signal (75.13\% for Qwen, 73.36\% for DeepSeek), only marginally above majority-class guessing (71.51\%).
49:DeepSeek shows a bimodal pattern ($n{=}3$): seeds 42 and 456 both achieve 99.33\% (8 errors each, fail recall = 100\%), while seed 123 yields 91.09\% (fail recall = 70.5\%), consistent with LoRA initialization sensitivity on the smaller model.
109:This two-factor decomposition is consistent across models: the VOTA cross-model gap (14.96pp, Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}) is confounded by training-time trace truncation on DeepSeek (\S\ref{sec:cross_model}), which amplifies information overload by removing diagnostic trace content from the majority of training samples.
153:\caption{Cross-model accuracy gap (Qwen $-$ DeepSeek) across trace conditions. Exception-based signals transfer across models (0.50pp gap), while VOTA is model-dependent (14.96pp gap).}
```

55	Second, we evaluate only two models at the 7B parameter scale; the relationship between model size and trace signal utility is unexplored.
56	Third, DeepSeek \texttt{exception\_only} exhibits seed-dependent variance: 2/3 seeds achieve 99.33\% while seed 123 yields 91.09\% ($\sigma{=}4.76$pp, $n{=}3$), attributable to LoRA initialization sensitivity (fail recall = 70.5\% for the outlier vs.\ 100\% for others).
57	Qwen shows perfect seed robustness ($\sigma{=}0$, $n{=}3$).
58	Whether training stabilization techniques (e.g., larger warmup ratio, LoRA rank adjustment) can eliminate DeepSeek's seed sensitivity is unexplored.
59	Other conditions use a single seed.
60	Fourth, the DeepSeek \texttt{full\_trace} condition was invalidated because the DeepSeek tokenizer produced sequences exceeding 2,048 tokens, causing the verdict label to be truncated during training; the model consequently failed to learn the classification task, and we report it as N/A rather than presenting potentially misleading results.
61	Fifth, the candidate solutions were generated by Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct, which also serves as one of our evaluation models.
62	This does not affect verdict prediction---the task is to classify a \emph{given} solution as pass or fail, not to generate solutions---but we note the overlap for transparency.
63	Moreover, the pass/fail distribution in the dataset is determined by the generating model's coding ability; a stronger or weaker generator would yield a different class balance, potentially affecting task difficulty and the generalizability of our accuracy estimates.
64	Finally, our analysis is limited to verdict prediction; whether exception dominance extends to other classification tasks (vulnerability detection, clone detection) or to generation tasks (program repair) is an open question.

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HHxKUizmfNgazpig54ZHc2`</summary>

```text
38	Post-hoc tokenizer analysis reveals a confound: measured on the training set using each model's tokenizer, the DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}759 tokens per VOTA sample versus 1{,}908 for Qwen, causing 91.7\% of DeepSeek VOTA training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window.
39	Unlike the DeepSeek \texttt{full\_trace} condition (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}, $^\ddag$), where right-truncation removed the verdict label entirely, the VOTA training framework preserves the verdict label but truncates the trace content (input), so the model trains with incomplete diagnostic information for the vast majority of the dataset.
40	The 14.96pp gap thus reflects both model capability differences and training-time trace truncation; the two factors cannot be disentangled in the current design.
41	However, a post-hoc group analysis provides directional evidence: partitioning DeepSeek VOTA test samples by whether their training-set counterparts exceeded the context window, intact samples ($N{=}99$) achieve 100\% accuracy while truncated samples ($N{=}1{,}091$) achieve 83.04\% (95\% CI [80.75\%, 85.24\%]; Fisher exact $p{<}0.001$), with all 185 errors concentrated in the fail class of the truncated group (fail recall $=$ 31.23\%).
42	In contrast, the \texttt{exception\_only} condition is unaffected by truncation because exception-based inputs are short enough to fit within the context window for both tokenizers, making its cross-model comparison clean.
43	Per-class analysis (Appendix~\ref{sec:per_class}) reveals the mechanism: DeepSeek VOTA achieves 100\% fail precision but only 45.43\% fail recall, systematically defaulting to \texttt{pass}---in contrast to Qwen VOTA, which achieves 100\% fail recall.
44	
45	The \texttt{no\_trace} baseline confirms that source code alone provides limited signal (75.13\% for Qwen, 73.36\% for DeepSeek), only marginally above majority-class guessing (71.51\%).
46	
47	Seed robustness diverges sharply across models.
```

85	
86	\subsection{Information Density Decomposition}
87	\label{sec:density}
88	
89	The gap between \texttt{full\_trace} (81.76\%) and VOTA (99.41\%) is 17.65pp.
90	We decompose this gap into two mechanisms by introducing an intermediate condition, \texttt{full\_trace\_label\_only}, which retains the complete trace as input but computes loss only on the verdict token, masking all trace tokens during training.
91	
92	\begin{table}[t]
93	\centering
94	\small
95	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
96	\toprule
97	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Acc (\%)} & \textbf{$\Delta$ vs.\ full\_trace} & \textbf{Gap explained} \\
98	\midrule
99	full\_trace & 81.76 & --- & --- \\
100	label\_only & 86.22 & +4.46pp & 26\% \\
101	VOTA & 99.41 & +17.65pp & 100\% \\
102	\bottomrule
103	\end{tabular}
104	\caption{Information density decomposition. The full\_trace$\to$VOTA gap decomposes into gradient dilution (26\%, addressed by masking trace tokens from the loss) and information overload (74\%, addressed by removing irrelevant trace lines via VOTA compression).}
105	\label{tab:density}
106	\end{table}
107	
108	As shown in Table~\ref{tab:density}, the decomposition attributes 26\% of the gap (+4.46pp, $p{<}0.0001$, bootstrap 95\% CI $[{+}2.35, {+}6.55]$) to \emph{gradient dilution}---the training signal is diluted across trace tokens that do not contribute to verdict prediction---and 74\% (+13.19pp) to \emph{information overload}---the model must process trace lines irrelevant to the fault at inference time.
109	This two-factor decomposition is consistent across models: the VOTA cross-model gap (14.96pp, Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}) is confounded by training-time trace truncation on DeepSeek (\S\ref{sec:cross_model}), which amplifies information overload by removing diagnostic trace content from the majority of training samples.
110	
111	\subsection{Calibration Analysis}
112	\label{sec:calibration}
113	
114	Beyond accuracy, we evaluate whether trace conditions affect prediction calibration, motivated by recent findings that code LLM confidence is often poorly calibrated \citep{spiess2025calibration}.
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\nWe evaluate our trace preprocessing pipeline across two research questions:\n(1)~Do Tier~1 exception signals generalize across models of different capability?\n(2)~What is the relative contribution of each trace component to verdict prediction accuracy?\nAll experiments use our trace--verdict dataset (\\S\\ref{sec:dataset}) with $N{=}1{,}190$ test samples; accuracy is reported with Wilson 95\\% confidence intervals unless stated otherwise.\nPairwise comparisons use paired bootstrap resampling ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed=42); all cross-condition differences reported below are significant at $p{<}0.0001$ unless noted otherwise.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Comparison}\n\\label{sec:cross_model}\n\nTo test whether exception-based trace compression transfers across model families, we compare Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct under four input conditions: \\texttt{exception\\_only} (Tier~1 exception events), VOTA (exception + assertion-point + loop compression), \\texttt{full\\_trace} (complete execution trace), and \\texttt{no\\_trace} (source code only).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Qwen-2.5-7B} & \\textbf{DeepSeek-6.7B} & \\textbf{Gap} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only & \\textbf{99.83}\\% & 99.33\\%$^\\dag$ & 0.50pp \\\\\nVOTA & 99.41\\% & 84.45\\% & 14.96pp \\\\\nVOTA\\_no\\_ae & 99.41\\%$^\\S$ & --- & --- \\\\\nfull\\_trace & 81.76\\% & N/A$^\\ddag$ & --- \\\\\nno\\_trace & 75.13\\% & 73.36\\% & 1.77pp \\\\\n\\midrule\nMajority baseline & 71.51\\% & 71.51\\% & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Cross-model accuracy ($N{=}1{,}190$). Qwen CIs: exception\\_only [99.58, 100.00], VOTA [98.82, 99.83], full\\_trace [79.70, 83.90], no\\_trace [72.77, 77.48]; DeepSeek CIs: VOTA [82.60, 86.40], no\\_trace [70.80, 75.70]. $^\\dag$DeepSeek \\texttt{exception\\_only} 3-seed results: 99.33\\% (seeds 42, 456; CI [98.82, 99.75]) and 91.09\\% (seed 123; CI [89.40, 92.60]); seed 123 is a LoRA initialization outlier (fail recall = 70.5\\% vs.\\ 100\\%). $^\\S$VOTA\\_no\\_ae evaluated on Qwen only; CI [98.90, 99.80]. $^\\ddag$DeepSeek full\\_trace was invalidated because the DeepSeek tokenizer produced sequences exceeding 2{,}048 tokens, causing the verdict label to be truncated during training; the model consequently failed to learn the classification task.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_model} reveals a striking asymmetry.\nUnder \\texttt{exception\\_only}, Qwen achieves 99.83\\% $\\pm$ 0.00 across three seeds, while DeepSeek shows a bimodal pattern: seeds 42 and 456 both achieve 99.33\\% (gap = 0.50pp), while seed 123 yields 91.09\\% (gap = 8.74pp), attributable to suboptimal LoRA initialization (fail recall = 70.5\\% vs.\\ 100\\% for other seeds).\nEven the outlier seed outperforms VOTA (84.45\\%) by 6.64pp, confirming exception events as the most effective component across models.\nUnder VOTA, the gap widens to 14.96pp: DeepSeek drops to 84.45\\% while Qwen remains at 99.41\\%.\nPost-hoc tokenizer analysis reveals a confound: measured on the training set using each model's tokenizer, the DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}759 tokens per VOTA sample versus 1{,}908 for Qwen, causing 91.7\\% of DeepSeek VOTA training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window.\nUnlike the DeepSeek \\texttt{full\\_trace} condition (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}, $^\\ddag$), where right-truncation removed the verdict label entirely, the VOTA training framework preserves the verdict label but truncates the trace content (input), so the model trains with incomplete diagnostic information for the vast majority of the dataset.\nThe 14.96pp gap thus reflects both model capability differences and training-time trace truncation; the two factors cannot be disentangled in the current design.\nHowever, a post-hoc group analysis provides directional evidence: partitioning DeepSeek VOTA test samples by whether their training-set counterparts exceeded the context window, intact samples ($N{=}99$) achieve 100\\% accuracy while truncated samples ($N{=}1{,}091$) achieve 83.04\\% (95\\% CI [80.75\\%, 85.24\\%]; Fisher exact $p{<}0.001$), with all 185 errors concentrated in the fail class of the truncated group (fail recall $=$ 31.23\\%).\nIn contrast, the \\texttt{exception\\_only} condition is unaffected by truncation because exception-based inputs are short enough to fit within the context window for both tokenizers, making its cross-model comparison clean.\nPer-class analysis (Appendix~\\ref{sec:per_class}) reveals the mechanism: DeepSeek VOTA achieves 100\\% fail precision but only 45.43\\% fail recall, systematically defaulting to \\texttt{pass}---in contrast to Qwen VOTA, which achieves 100\\% fail recall.\n\nThe \\texttt{no\\_trace} baseline confirms that source code alone provides limited signal (75.13\\% for Qwen, 73.36\\% for DeepSeek), only marginally above majority-class guessing (71.51\\%).\n\nSeed robustness diverges sharply across models.\nQwen \\texttt{exception\\_only} produces identical accuracy of 99.83\\% across all three seeds ($\\sigma{=}0$, $n{=}3$, exactly two errors each).\nDeepSeek shows a bimodal pattern ($n{=}3$): seeds 42 and 456 both achieve 99.33\\% (8 errors each, fail recall = 100\\%), while seed 123 yields 91.09\\% (fail recall = 70.5\\%), consistent with LoRA initialization sensitivity on the smaller model.\nBecause the \\texttt{exception\\_only} test set contains 48.99\\% duplicate inputs (607 unique out of 1,190; \\S\\ref{sec:dataset}), the reported CIs assume $n{=}1{,}190$ independent trials.\nUnder deduplication ($n{=}607$), Qwen's two errors yield 99.67\\% accuracy (Wilson CI [98.81, 99.91]), confirming that the result is robust to the effective sample size.\n\n\\subsection{Component Ablation}\n\\label{sec:ablation}\n\nWe decompose the VOTA pipeline into individual components on Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42).\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} arranges conditions into a two-tier hierarchy: Tier~1 signals (\\texttt{ae\\_only}, \\texttt{exception\\_only}) that individually achieve ${\\geq}$99.8\\% accuracy, and Tier~2 signals (\\texttt{loop\\_only}) that provide moderate improvement over the no-trace baseline.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Components} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{Tier} \\\\\n\\midrule\nae\\_only & assertion-point & \\textbf{99.92} & [99.75, 100.00] & 1 \\\\\nexception\\_only & exception events & 99.83 & [99.58, 100.00] & 1 \\\\\nVOTA & exc+ae+loop & 99.41 & [98.82, 99.83] & --- \\\\\nVOTA\\_no\\_ae & exc+loop & 99.41 & [98.90, 99.80] & --- \\\\\nloop\\_only & loop compression & 85.63 & [83.53, 87.56] & 2 \\\\\nfull\\_trace & complete trace & 81.76 & [79.70, 83.90] & --- \\\\\nno\\_trace & none & 75.13 & [72.77, 77.48] & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Component ablation on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). Tier~1 signals individually achieve near-perfect accuracy; combining them (VOTA) slightly dilutes performance.}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\nTwo findings stand out.\nFirst, the Tier~1 signals are individually sufficient: \\texttt{ae\\_only} (99.92\\%) and \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%) are not distinguishable ($p{=}0.80$, paired bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$), and both exceed VOTA (99.41\\%, $p{=}0.01$ for \\texttt{exception\\_only} vs.\\ VOTA, paired bootstrap).\nThis implies that combining signals in VOTA introduces minor noise rather than complementary information.\nSecond, removing assertion points from VOTA has no effect (VOTA\\_no\\_ae = VOTA = 99.41\\%), confirming that within the VOTA mixture, exception events dominate the prediction signal.\n\nTier~2 signal \\texttt{loop\\_only} (85.63\\%) improves over \\texttt{no\\_trace} (75.13\\%) by 10.50pp ($p{<}0.0001$, bootstrap 95\\% CI $[{+}8.24, {+}12.86]$), showing that loop compression captures some execution semantics, but far less than exception events.\n\n\\subsection{Information Density Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:density}\n\nThe gap between \\texttt{full\\_trace} (81.76\\%) and VOTA (99.41\\%) is 17.65pp.\nWe decompose this gap into two mechanisms by introducing an intermediate condition, \\texttt{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only}, which retains the complete trace as input but computes loss only on the verdict token, masking all trace tokens during training.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ full\\_trace} & \\textbf{Gap explained} \\\\\n\\midrule\nfull\\_trace & 81.76 & --- & --- \\\\\nlabel\\_only & 86.22 & +4.46pp & 26\\% \\\\\nVOTA & 99.41 & +17.65pp & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Information density decomposition. The full\\_trace$\\to$VOTA gap decomposes into gradient dilution (26\\%, addressed by masking trace tokens from the loss) and information overload (74\\%, addressed by removing irrelevant trace lines via VOTA compression).}\n\\label{tab:density}\n\\end{table}\n\nAs shown in Table~\\ref{tab:density}, the decomposition attributes 26\\% of the gap (+4.46pp, $p{<}0.0001$, bootstrap 95\\% CI $[{+}2.35, {+}6.55]$) to \\emph{gradient dilution}---the training signal is diluted across trace tokens that do not contribute to verdict prediction---and 74\\% (+13.19pp) to \\emph{information overload}---the model must process trace lines irrelevant to the fault at inference time.\nThis two-factor decomposition is consistent across models: the VOTA cross-model gap (14.96pp, Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}) is confounded by training-time trace truncation on DeepSeek (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}), which amplifies information overload by removing diagnostic trace content from the majority of training samples.\n\n\\subsection{Calibration Analysis}\n\\label{sec:calibration}\n\nBeyond accuracy, we evaluate whether trace conditions affect prediction calibration, motivated by recent findings that code LLM confidence is often poorly calibrated \\citep{spiess2025calibration}.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:calibration} shows reliability diagrams for four conditions on Qwen-2.5-7B.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{ECE $\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{Brier $\\downarrow$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nae\\_only & \\textbf{0.0004} & \\textbf{0.0008} \\\\\nexception\\_only & 0.003 & 0.0013 \\\\\nVOTA & 0.005 & 0.005 \\\\\nfull\\_trace & 0.096 & 0.144 \\\\\nno\\_trace & 0.171 & 0.197 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Calibration metrics on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). ECE = expected calibration error; Brier = Brier score. Lower is better for both.}\n\\label{tab:calibration}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:calibration} shows that calibration quality mirrors the Tier~1/2 hierarchy.\nTier~1 conditions (\\texttt{ae\\_only}: ECE=0.0004; \\texttt{exception\\_only}: ECE=0.003) produce near-perfectly calibrated predictions, while \\texttt{full\\_trace} (ECE=0.096) and \\texttt{no\\_trace} (ECE=0.171) exhibit substantial miscalibration.\nVOTA (ECE=0.005) is well-calibrated but slightly worse than either Tier~1 component alone, reinforcing the pattern that combining trace signals dilutes rather than improves the prediction signal.\n\nThe Brier scores tell a consistent story: \\texttt{ae\\_only} (0.0008) and \\texttt{exception\\_only} (0.0013) achieve near-zero scores, while \\texttt{no\\_trace} (0.197) indicates substantial miscalibration.\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:cross_model_gap} visualizes the cross-model gap pattern, showing that exception-based conditions maintain tight cross-model agreement while VOTA and lower-tier conditions diverge.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig2_calibration}\n\\caption{Reliability diagrams for four trace conditions on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). The Tier~1 condition (\\texttt{exception\\_only}) clusters near the diagonal (well-calibrated), while \\texttt{full\\_trace} and \\texttt{no\\_trace} exhibit systematic overconfidence.}\n\\label{fig:calibration}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_cross_model_gap}\n\\caption{Cross-model accuracy gap (Qwen $-$ DeepSeek) across trace conditions. Exception-based signals transfer across models (0.50pp gap), while VOTA is model-dependent (14.96pp gap).}\n\\label{fig:cross_model_gap}\n\\end{figure}",

Fisher exact $p{<}0.001$), with all 185 errors concentrated in the fail class of the truncated group (fail recall $=$ 31.23\\%).\nIn contrast, the \\texttt{exception\\_only} condition is unaffected by truncation because exception-based inputs are short enough to fit within the context window for both tokenizers, making its cross-model comparison clean.\nPer-class analysis (Appendix~\\ref{sec:per_class}) reveals the mechanism: DeepSeek VOTA achieves 100\\% fail precision but only 45.43\\% fail recall, systematically defaulting to \\texttt{pass}---in contrast to Qwen VOTA, which achieves 100\\% fail recall.\n\nThe \\texttt{no\\_trace} baseline confirms that source code alone provides limited signal (75.13\\% for Qwen, 73.36\\% for DeepSeek), only marginally above majority-class guessing (71.51\\%).\n\nSeed robustness diverges sharply across models.\nQwen \\texttt{exception\\_only} produces identical accuracy of 99.83\\% across all three seeds ($\\sigma{=}0$, $n{=}3$, exactly two errors each).\nDeepSeek shows a bimodal pattern ($n{=}3$): seeds 42 and 456 both achieve 99.33\\% (8 errors each, fail recall = 100\\%), while seed 123 yields 91.09\\% (fail recall = 70.5\\%), consistent with LoRA initialization sensitivity on the smaller model.\nBecause the \\texttt{exception\\_only} test set contains 48.99\\% duplicate inputs (607 unique out of 1,190; \\S\\ref{sec:dataset}), the reported CIs assume $n{=}1{,}190$ independent trials.\nUnder deduplication ($n{=}607$), Qwen's two errors yield 99.67\\% accuracy (Wilson CI [98.81, 99.91]), confirming that the result is robust to the effective sample size.\n\n\\subsection{Component Ablation}\n\\label{sec:ablation}\n\nWe decompose the VOTA pipeline into individual components on Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42).\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} arranges conditions into a two-tier hierarchy: Tier~1 signals (\\texttt{ae\\_only}, \\texttt{exception\\_only}) that individually achieve ${\\geq}$99.8\\% accuracy, and Tier~2 signals (\\texttt{loop\\_only}) that provide moderate improvement over the no-trace baseline.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Components} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{Tier} \\\\\n\\midrule\nae\\_only & assertion-point & \\textbf{99.92} & [99.75, 100.00] & 1 \\\\\nexception\\_only & exception events & 99.83 & [99.58, 100.00] & 1 \\\\\nVOTA & exc+ae+loop & 99.41 & [98.82, 99.83] & --- \\\\\nVOTA\\_no\\_ae & exc+loop & 99.41 & [98.90, 99.80] & --- \\\\\nloop\\_only & loop compression & 85.63 & [83.53, 87.56] & 2 \\\\\nfull\\_trace & complete trace & 81.76 & [79.70, 83.90] & --- \\\\\nno\\_trace & none & 75.13 & [72.77, 77.48] & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Component ablation on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). Tier~1 signals individually achieve near-perfect accuracy; combining them (VOTA) slightly dilutes performance.}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\nTwo findings stand out.\nFirst, the Tier~1 signals are individually sufficient: \\texttt{ae\\_only} (99.92\\%) and \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%) are not distinguishable ($p{=}0.80$, paired bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$), and both exceed VOTA (99.41\\%, $p{=}0.01$ for \\texttt{exception\\_only} vs.\\ VOTA, paired bootstrap).\nThis implies that combining signals in VOTA introduces minor noise rather than complementary information.\nSecond, removing assertion points from VOTA has no effect (VOTA\\_no\\_ae = VOTA = 99.41\\%), confirming that within the VOTA mixture, exception events dominate the prediction signal.\n\nTier~2 signal \\texttt{loop\\_only} (85.63\\%) improves over \\texttt{no\\_trace} (75.13\\%) by 10.50pp ($p{<}0.0001$, bootstrap 95\\% CI $[{+}8.24, {+}12.86]$), showing that loop compression captures some execution semantics, but far less than exception events.\n\n\\subsection{Information Density Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:density}\n\nThe gap between \\texttt{full\\_trace} (81.76\\%) and VOTA (99.41\\%) is 17.65pp.\nWe decompose this gap into two mechanisms by introducing an intermediate condition, \\texttt{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only}, which retains the complete trace as input but computes loss only on the verdict token, masking all trace tokens during training.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ full\\_trace} & \\textbf{Gap explained} \\\\\n\\midrule\nfull\\_trace & 81.76 & --- & --- \\\\\nlabel\\_only & 86.22 & +4.46pp & 26\\% \\\\\nVOTA & 99.41 & +17.65pp & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Information density decomposition. The full\\_trace$\\to$VOTA gap decomposes into gradient dilution (26\\%, addressed by masking trace tokens from the loss) and information overload (74\\%, addressed by removing irrelevant trace lines via VOTA compression).}\n\\label{tab:density}\n\\end{table}\n\nAs shown in Table~\\ref{tab:density}, the decomposition attributes 26\\% of the gap (+4.46pp, $p{<}0.0001$, bootstrap 95\\% CI $[{+}2.35, {+}6.55]$) to \\emph{gradient dilution}---the training signal is diluted across trace tokens that do not contribute to verdict prediction---and 74\\% (+13.19pp) to \\emph{information overload}---the model must process trace lines irrelevant to the fault at inference time.\nThis two-factor decomposition is consistent across models: the VOTA cross-model gap (14.96pp, Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}) is confounded by training-time trace truncation on DeepSeek (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}), which amplifies information overload by removing diagnostic trace content from the majority of training samples.\n\n\\subsection{Calibration Analysis}\n\\label{sec:calibration}\n\nBeyond accuracy, we evaluate whether trace conditions affect prediction calibration, motivated by recent findings that code LLM confidence is often poorly calibrated \\citep{spiess2025calibration}.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:calibration} shows reliability diagrams for four conditions on Qwen-2.5-7B.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{ECE $\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{Brier $\\downarrow$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nae\\_only & \\textbf{0.0004} & \\textbf{0.0008} \\\\\nexception\\_only & 0.003 & 0.0013 \\\\\nVOTA & 0.005 & 0.005 \\\\\nfull\\_trace & 0.096 & 0.144 \\\\\nno\\_trace & 0.171 & 0.197 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Calibration metrics on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). ECE = expected calibration error; Brier = Brier score. Lower is better for both.}\n\\label{tab:calibration}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:calibration} shows that calibration quality mirrors the Tier~1/2 hierarchy.\nTier~1 conditions (\\texttt{ae\\_only}: ECE=0.0004; \\texttt{exception\\_only}: ECE=0.003) produce near-perfectly calibrated predictions, while \\texttt{full\\_trace} (ECE=0.096) and \\texttt{no\\_trace} (ECE=0.171) exhibit substantial miscalibration.\nVOTA (ECE=0.005) is well-calibrated but slightly worse than either Tier~1 component alone, reinforcing the pattern that combining trace signals dilutes rather than improves the prediction signal.\n\nThe Brier scores tell a consistent story: \\texttt{ae\\_only} (0.0008) and \\texttt{exception\\_only} (0.0013) achieve near-zero scores, while \\texttt{no\\_trace} (0.197) indicates substantial miscalibration.\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:cross_model_gap} visualizes the cross-model gap pattern, showing that exception-based conditions maintain tight cross-model agreement while VOTA and lower-tier conditions diverge.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig2_calibration}\n\\caption{Reliability diagrams for four trace conditions on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). The Tier~1 condition (\\texttt{exception\\_only}) clusters near the diagonal (well-calibrated), while \\texttt{full\\_trace} and \\texttt{no\\_trace} exhibit systematic overconfidence.}\n\\label{fig:calibration}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_cross_model_gap}\n\\caption{Cross-model accuracy gap (Qwen $-$ DeepSeek) across trace conditions. Exception-based signals transfer across models (0.50pp gap), while VOTA is model-dependent (14.96pp gap).}\n\\label{fig:cross_model_gap}\n\\end{figure}",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\nWe evaluate our trace preprocessing pipeline across two research questions:\n(1)~Do Tier~1 exception signals generalize across models of different capability?\n(2)~What is the relative contribution of each trace component to verdict prediction accuracy?\nAll experiments use our trace--verdict dataset (\\S\\ref{sec:dataset}) with $N{=}1{,}190$ test samples; accuracy is reported with Wilson 95\\% confidence intervals unless stated otherwise.\nPairwise comparisons use paired bootstrap resampling ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed=42); all cross-condition differences reported below are significant at $p{<}0.0001$ unless noted otherwise.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Comparison}\n\\label{sec:cross_model}\n\nTo test whether exception-based trace compression transfers across model families, we compare Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct under four input conditions: \\texttt{exception\\_only} (Tier~1 exception events), VOTA (exception + assertion-point + loop compression), \\texttt{full\\_trace} (complete execution trace), and \\texttt{no\\_trace} (source code only).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Qwen-2.5-7B} & \\textbf{DeepSeek-6.7B} & \\textbf{Gap} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only & \\textbf{99.83}\\% & 99.33\\%$^\\dag$ & 0.50pp \\\\\nVOTA & 99.41\\% & 84.45\\% & 14.96pp \\\\\nVOTA\\_no\\_ae & 99.41\\%$^\\S$ & --- & --- \\\\\nfull\\_trace & 81.76\\% & 79.16\\% & 2.60pp \\\\\nno\\_trace & 75.13\\% & 73.36\\% & 1.77pp \\\\\n\\midrule\nMajority baseline & 71.51\\% & 71.51\\% & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Cross-model accuracy ($N{=}1{,}190$). Qwen CIs: exception\\_only [99.58, 100.00], VOTA [98.82, 99.83], full\\_trace [79.70, 83.90], no\\_trace [72.77, 77.48]; DeepSeek CIs: full\\_trace [76.80, 81.30], VOTA [82.60, 86.40], no\\_trace [70.80, 75.70]. $^\\dag$DeepSeek \\texttt{exception\\_only} 3-seed results: 99.33\\% (seeds 42, 456; CI [98.82, 99.75]) and 91.09\\% (seed 123; CI [89.40, 92.60]); seed 123 is a LoRA initialization outlier (fail recall = 70.5\\% vs.\\ 100\\%). $^\\S$VOTA\\_no\\_ae evaluated on Qwen only; CI [98.90, 99.80]. $^\\ddag$DeepSeek full\\_trace was invalidated because the DeepSeek tokenizer produced sequences exceeding 2{,}048 tokens, causing the verdict label to be truncated during training; the model consequently failed to learn the classification task.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_model} reveals a striking asymmetry.\nUnder \\texttt{exception\\_only}, Qwen achieves 99.83\\% $\\pm$ 0.00 across three seeds, while DeepSeek shows a bimodal pattern: seeds 42 and 456 both achieve 99.33\\% (gap = 0.50pp), while seed 123 yields 91.09\\% (gap = 8.74pp), attributable to suboptimal LoRA initialization (fail recall = 70.5\\% vs.\\ 100\\% for other seeds).\nEven the outlier seed outperforms VOTA (84.45\\%) by 6.64pp, confirming exception events as the most effective component across models.\nUnder VOTA, the gap widens to 14.96pp: DeepSeek drops to 84.45\\% while Qwen remains at 99.41\\%.\nPost-hoc tokenizer analysis reveals a confound: measured on the training set using each model's tokenizer, the DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}759 tokens per VOTA sample versus 1{,}908 for Qwen, causing 91.7\\% of DeepSeek VOTA training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window.\nUnlike the DeepSeek \\texttt{full\\_trace} condition (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}, $^\\ddag$), where right-truncation removed the verdict label entirely, the VOTA training framework preserves the verdict label but truncates the trace content (input), so the model trains with incomplete diagnostic information for the vast majority of the dataset.\nThe 14.96pp gap thus reflects both model capability differences and training-time trace truncation; the two factors cannot be disentangled in the current design.\nHowever, a post-hoc group analysis provides directional evidence: partitioning DeepSeek VOTA test samples by whether their training-set counterparts exceeded the context window, intact samples ($N{=}99$) achieve 100\\% accuracy while truncated samples ($N{=}1{,}091$) achieve 83.04\\% (95\\% CI [80.75\\%, 85.24\\%];     "newString": "The VOTA training framework preserves the verdict label but truncates the trace content (input), so the model trains with incomplete diagnostic information for the vast majority of the dataset.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\nWe evaluate our trace preprocessing pipeline across two research questions:\n(1)~Do Tier~1 exception signals generalize across models of different capability?\n(2)~What is the relative contribution of each trace component to verdict prediction accuracy?\nAll experiments use our trace--verdict dataset (\\S\\ref{sec:dataset}) with $N{=}1{,}190$ test samples; accuracy is reported with Wilson 95\\% confidence intervals unless stated otherwise.\nPairwise comparisons use paired bootstrap resampling ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed=42); all cross-condition differences reported below are significant at $p{<}0.0001$ unless noted otherwise.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Comparison}\n\\label{sec:cross_model}\n\nTo test whether exception-based trace compression transfers across model families, we compare Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct under four input conditions: \\texttt{exception\\_only} (Tier~1 exception events), VOTA (exception + assertion-point + loop compression), \\texttt{full\\_trace} (complete execution trace), and \\texttt{no\\_trace} (source code only).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Qwen-2.5-7B} & \\textbf{DeepSeek-6.7B} & \\textbf{Gap} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only & \\textbf{99.83}\\% & 99.33\\%$^\\dag$ & 0.50pp \\\\\nVOTA & 99.41\\% & 84.45\\% & 14.96pp \\\\\nVOTA\\_no\\_ae & 99.41\\%$^\\S$ & --- & --- \\\\\nfull\\_trace & 81.76\\% & 79.16\\% & 2.60pp \\\\\nno\\_trace & 75.13\\% & 73.36\\% & 1.77pp \\\\\n\\midrule\nMajority baseline & 71.51\\% & 71.51\\% & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Cross-model accuracy ($N{=}1{,}190$). Qwen CIs: exception\\_only [99.58, 100.00], VOTA [98.82, 99.83], full\\_trace [79.70, 83.90], no\\_trace [72.77, 77.48]; DeepSeek CIs: full\\_trace [76.80, 81.30], VOTA [82.60, 86.40], no\\_trace [70.80, 75.70]. $^\\dag$DeepSeek \\texttt{exception\\_only} 3-seed results: 99.33\\% (seeds 42, 456; CI [98.82, 99.75]) and 91.09\\% (seed 123; CI [89.40, 92.60]); seed 123 is a LoRA initialization outlier (fail recall = 70.5\\% vs.\\ 100\\%). $^\\S$VOTA\\_no\\_ae evaluated on Qwen only; CI [98.90, 99.80].}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_model} reveals a striking asymmetry.\nUnder \\texttt{exception\\_only}, Qwen achieves 99.83\\% $\\pm$ 0.00 across three seeds, while DeepSeek shows a bimodal pattern: seeds 42 and 456 both achieve 99.33\\% (gap = 0.50pp), while seed 123 yields 91.09\\% (gap = 8.74pp), attributable to suboptimal LoRA initialization (fail recall = 70.5\\% vs.\\ 100\\% for other seeds).\nEven the outlier seed outperforms VOTA (84.45\\%) by 6.64pp, confirming exception events as the most effective component across models.\nUnder VOTA, the gap widens to 14.96pp: DeepSeek drops to 84.45\\% while Qwen remains at 99.41\\%.\nPost-hoc tokenizer analysis reveals a confound: measured on the training set using each model's tokenizer, the DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}759 tokens per VOTA sample versus 1{,}908 for Qwen, causing 91.7\\% of DeepSeek VOTA training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window.\nUnlike the DeepSeek \\texttt{full\\_trace} condition (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}, $^\\ddag$), where right-truncation removed the verdict label entirely, the VOTA training framework preserves the verdict label but truncates the trace content (input), so the model trains with incomplete diagnostic information for the vast majority of the dataset.\nThe 14.96pp gap thus reflects both model capability differences and training-time trace truncation; the two factors cannot be disentangled in the current design.\nHowever, a post-hoc group analysis provides directional evidence: partitioning DeepSeek VOTA test samples by whether their training-set counterparts exceeded the context window, intact samples ($N{=}99$) achieve 100\\% accuracy while truncated samples ($N{=}1{,}091$) achieve 83.04\\% (95\\% CI [80.75\\%, 85.24\\%]; Fisher exact $p{<}0.001$), with all 185 errors concentrated in the fail class of the truncated group (fail recall $=$ 31.23\\%).\nIn contrast, the \\texttt{exception\\_only} condition is unaffected by truncation because exception-based inputs are short enough to fit within the context window for both tokenizers, making its cross-model comparison clean.\nPer-class analysis (Appendix~\\ref{sec:per_class}) reveals the mechanism: DeepSeek VOTA achieves 100\\% fail precision but only 45.43\\% fail recall, systematically defaulting to \\texttt{pass}---in contrast to Qwen VOTA, which achieves 100\\% fail recall.\n\nThe \\texttt{no\\_trace} baseline confirms that source code alone provides limited signal (75.13\\% for Qwen, 73.36\\% for DeepSeek), only marginally above majority-class guessing (71.51\\%).\n\nSeed robustness diverges sharply across models.\nQwen \\texttt{exception\\_only} produces identical accuracy of 99.83\\% across all three seeds ($\\sigma{=}0$, $n{=}3$, exactly two errors each).\nDeepSeek shows a bimodal pattern ($n{=}3$): seeds 42 and 456 both achieve 99.33\\% (8 errors each, fail recall = 100\\%), while seed 123 yields 91.09\\% (fail recall = 70.5\\%), consistent with LoRA initialization sensitivity on the smaller model.\nBecause the \\texttt{exception\\_only} test set contains 48.99\\% duplicate inputs (607 unique out of 1,190; \\S\\ref{sec:dataset}), the reported CIs assume $n{=}1{,}190$ independent trials.\nUnder deduplication ($n{=}607$), Qwen's two errors yield 99.67\\% accuracy (Wilson CI [98.81, 99.91]), confirming that the result is robust to the effective sample size.\n\n\\subsection{Component Ablation}\n\\label{sec:ablation}\n\nWe decompose the VOTA pipeline into individual components on Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42).\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} arranges conditions into a two-tier hierarchy: Tier~1 signals (\\texttt{ae\\_only}, \\texttt{exception\\_only}) that individually achieve ${\\geq}$99.8\\% accuracy, and Tier~2 signals (\\texttt{loop\\_only}) that provide moderate improvement over the no-trace baseline.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Components} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{Tier} \\\\\n\\midrule\nae\\_only & assertion-point & \\textbf{99.92} & [99.75, 100.00] & 1 \\\\\nexception\\_only & exception events & 99.83 & [99.58, 100.00] & 1 \\\\\nVOTA & exc+ae+loop & 99.41 & [98.82, 99.83] & --- \\\\\nVOTA\\_no\\_ae & exc+loop & 99.41 & [98.90, 99.80] & --- \\\\\nloop\\_only & loop compression & 85.63 & [83.53, 87.56] & 2 \\\\\nfull\\_trace & complete trace & 81.76 & [79.70, 83.90] & --- \\\\\nno\\_trace & none & 75.13 & [72.77, 77.48] & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Component ablation on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). Tier~1 signals individually achieve near-perfect accuracy; combining them (VOTA) slightly dilutes performance.}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\nTwo findings stand out.\nFirst, the Tier~1 signals are individually sufficient: \\texttt{ae\\_only} (99.92\\%) and \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%) are not distinguishable ($p{=}0.80$, paired bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$), and both exceed VOTA (99.41\\%, $p{=}0.01$ for \\texttt{exception\\_only} vs.\\ VOTA, paired bootstrap).\nThis implies that combining signals in VOTA introduces minor noise rather than complementary information.\nSecond, removing assertion points from VOTA has no effect (VOTA\\_no\\_ae = VOTA = 99.41\\%), confirming that within the VOTA mixture, exception events dominate the prediction signal.\n\nTier~2 signal \\texttt{loop\\_only} (85.63\\%) improves over \\texttt{no\\_trace} (75.13\\%) by 10.50pp ($p{<}0.0001$, bootstrap 95\\% CI $[{+}8.24, {+}12.86]$), showing that loop compression captures some execution semantics, but far less than exception events.\n\n\\subsection{Information Density Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:density}\n\nThe gap between \\texttt{full\\_trace} (81.76\\%) and VOTA (99.41\\%) is 17.65pp.\nWe decompose this gap into two mechanisms by introducing an intermediate condition, \\texttt{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only}, which retains the complete trace as input but computes loss only on the verdict token, masking all trace tokens during training.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ full\\_trace} & \\textbf{Gap explained} \\\\\n\\midrule\nfull\\_trace & 81.76 & --- & --- \\\\\nlabel\\_only & 86.22 & +4.46pp & 26\\% \\\\\nVOTA & 99.41 & +17.65pp & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Information density decomposition. The full\\_trace$\\to$VOTA gap decomposes into gradient dilution (26\\%, addressed by masking trace tokens from the loss) and information overload (74\\%, addressed by removing irrelevant trace lines via VOTA compression).}\n\\label{tab:density}\n\\end{table}\n\nAs shown in Table~\\ref{tab:density}, the decomposition attributes 26\\% of the gap (+4.46pp, $p{<}0.0001$, bootstrap 95\\% CI $[{+}2.35, {+}6.55]$) to \\emph{gradient dilution}---the training signal is diluted across trace tokens that do not contribute to verdict prediction---and 74\\% (+13.19pp) to \\emph{information overload}---the model must process trace lines irrelevant to the fault at inference time.\nThis two-factor decomposition is consistent across models: the VOTA cross-model gap (14.96pp, Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}) is confounded by training-time trace truncation on DeepSeek (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}), which amplifies information overload by removing diagnostic trace content from the majority of training samples.\n\n\\subsection{Calibration Analysis}\n\\label{sec:calibration}\n\nBeyond accuracy, we evaluate whether trace conditions affect prediction calibration, motivated by recent findings that code LLM confidence is often poorly calibrated \\citep{spiess2025calibration}.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:calibration} shows reliability diagrams for four conditions on Qwen-2.5-7B.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{ECE $\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{Brier $\\downarrow$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nae\\_only & \\textbf{0.0004} & \\textbf{0.0008} \\\\\nexception\\_only & 0.003 & 0.0013 \\\\\nVOTA & 0.005 & 0.005 \\\\\nfull\\_trace & 0.096 & 0.144 \\\\\nno\\_trace & 0.171 & 0.197 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Calibration metrics on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). ECE = expected calibration error; Brier = Brier score. Lower is better for both.}\n\\label{tab:calibration}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:calibration} shows that calibration quality mirrors the Tier~1/2 hierarchy.\nTier~1 conditions (\\texttt{ae\\_only}: ECE=0.0004; \\texttt{exception\\_only}: ECE=0.003) produce near-perfectly calibrated predictions, while \\texttt{full\\_trace} (ECE=0.096) and \\texttt{no\\_trace} (ECE=0.171) exhibit substantial miscalibration.\nVOTA (ECE=0.005) is well-calibrated but slightly worse than either Tier~1 component alone, reinforcing the pattern that combining trace signals dilutes rather than improves the prediction signal.\n\nThe Brier scores tell a consistent story: \\texttt{ae\\_only} (0.0008) and \\texttt{exception\\_only} (0.0013) achieve near-zero scores, while \\texttt{no\\_trace} (0.197) indicates substantial miscalibration.\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:cross_model_gap} visualizes the cross-model gap pattern, showing that exception-based conditions maintain tight cross-model agreement while VOTA and lower-tier conditions diverge.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig2_calibration}\n\\caption{Reliability diagrams for four trace conditions on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). The Tier~1 condition (\\texttt{exception\\_only}) clusters near the diagonal (well-calibrated), while \\texttt{full\\_trace} and \\texttt{no\\_trace} exhibit systematic overconfidence.}\n\\label{fig:calibration}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_cross_model_gap}\n\\caption{Cross-model accuracy gap (Qwen $-$ DeepSeek) across trace conditions. Exception-based signals transfer across models (0.50pp gap), while VOTA is model-dependent (14.96pp gap).}\n\\label{fig:cross_model_gap}\n\\end{figure}",

  "tool_use_result": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_4/projects/trace-verdict-study/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
    "oldString": "Fourth, the DeepSeek \\texttt{full\\_trace} condition was invalidated because the DeepSeek tokenizer produced sequences exceeding 2,048 tokens, causing the verdict label to be truncated during training; the model consequently failed to learn the classification task, and we report it as N/A rather than presenting potentially misleading results.",
    "newString": "Fourth, the initial DeepSeek \\texttt{full\\_trace} run was invalidated by context overflow (the DeepSeek tokenizer produced sequences exceeding 2{,}048 tokens, truncating the verdict label); a re-run with extended context yields 79.16\\% (CI [76.80\\%, 81.30\\%]), 2.60pp below Qwen (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Why Exception Events Dominate}\n\nVerdict prediction is fundamentally a fault detection task: the model must determine whether execution deviated from expected behavior.\nException events are the most explicit signal of such deviation---structured diagnostic moments that encode the nature and location of a fault.\nUnlike loop compressions or assertion-point values, which require the model to integrate information across multiple trace elements, exception events provide a self-contained verdict cue.\nThis explains why \\texttt{exception\\_only} matches or exceeds VOTA: adding non-exception trace components dilutes the model's attention on the diagnostic signal rather than providing complementary information.\n\nWe acknowledge that exception-based verdict prediction is, at its core, fault indicator presence detection: the model learns to map the presence or absence of a structured error message to a binary label, which is close to trivially solvable for any instruction-tuned LLM.\nThe Tier~1 accuracy of ${\\geq}$99.8\\% is therefore unsurprising in isolation.\nThe contribution of this finding lies not in demonstrating that a strong signal yields high accuracy, but in two less obvious results: (a)~adding more trace information---loop compressions, assertion-point values---actively \\emph{hurts} performance (VOTA $<$ \\texttt{exception\\_only}, Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}), quantifying an information density dilution effect that has not been previously measured; and (b)~this dilution is confounded by tokenizer-driven trace truncation on DeepSeek: the DeepSeek tokenizer produces longer sequences than Qwen for identical inputs, causing the majority of VOTA training samples to exceed the context window (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}); the verdict label is preserved, but the trace content is truncated, depriving the model of diagnostic information and contributing to the collapse of DeepSeek VOTA fail recall to 45.43\\% (Table~\\ref{tab:per_class}) while Qwen maintains 100\\%.\n\n\\subsection{Simplicity as Relative Robustness}\n\nThe cross-model gap pattern (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}) suggests a design principle: \\emph{trace representations should match the model capability floor, not the ceiling.}\nException events are consistently the most effective component on both models, and their structural simplicity---a fixed-format error message---ensures that the relative advantage over richer representations holds across all seeds.\nTwo of three DeepSeek seeds match Qwen-level accuracy (99.33\\%); the outlier (seed 123, 91.09\\%) is attributable to LoRA initialization landing in a suboptimal basin (fail recall = 70.5\\%), not to a failure of the exception signal itself---even this outlier outperforms VOTA (84.45\\%) by 6.64pp.\nThe VOTA cross-model gap is confounded by two factors: (1)~tokenizer efficiency---the DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}759 tokens per VOTA sample versus Qwen's 1{,}908, causing 91.7\\% of training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window, truncating trace content while preserving the verdict label---and (2)~base model capability differences, consistent with findings that SFT generalization depends on pretrain quality \\citep{yang2024generalization}.\nThe two factors cannot be disentangled in the current experimental design.\nOur \\texttt{exception\\_only} design sidesteps the truncation confound entirely by keeping inputs short (median 314 tokens), making its cross-model comparison clean and the ``capability floor'' principle directly testable.\nThe practical implication is that trace-based systems should default to the simplest sufficient signal; for smaller models, training stability (multi-seed selection, warmup schedules) is a secondary but addressable concern.\n\n\\subsection{Training Dynamics and Loss Decoupling}\n\nThe DeepSeek \\texttt{exception\\_only} results reveal two instructive training dynamics.\nFirst, epoch-level analysis shows mild degradation in 2/3 seeds: seed 42 peaks at 99.83\\% after epoch~1 but settles at 99.33\\% by epoch~3 ($-$0.50pp), seed 123 degrades from 91.43\\% to 91.09\\% ($-$0.34pp), while seed 456 shows no epoch degradation (99.33\\% at both epoch~1 and epoch~3).\nEarly stopping may yield marginal gains for some seeds, but the epoch effect is small (${\\leq}$0.5pp) compared to the seed effect (8.24pp between seeds 42 and 123).\n\nSecond, seeds 123 and 456 converge to nearly identical final training losses (0.122 and 0.123 respectively), yet their accuracies differ by 8.24pp (91.09\\% vs.\\ 99.33\\%).\nThe critical difference lies in fail recall: seeds 42 and 456 achieve 100\\%, while seed 123 achieves only 70.5\\%---the LoRA initialization in seed 123 fails to learn the exception$\\to$fail mapping for a substantial fraction of failing samples, even though the per-token loss appears converged.\nThis demonstrates that token-level training loss can mask fundamental differences in sample-level classification ability, suggesting a parallel to the eval-loss/accuracy decoupling observed in DeepSeek VOTA (where eval loss rose 65\\% from epoch 1 to 3 while accuracy improved by 6.3pp) at the training phase.\nThe practical recommendation is twofold: (1)~use task-specific metrics (accuracy, fail recall) for checkpoint and seed selection rather than training or evaluation loss, and (2)~for deployment on smaller models, train multiple seeds and select the best, as a single run may not reliably converge to the correct classification boundary.\n\nThe contrast with VOTA is instructive: DeepSeek VOTA accuracy monotonically increases from 78.15\\% to 84.45\\% across epochs, but is consistent with a performance limit associated with the high trace truncation rate (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model})---the model progressively extracts signal from samples with intact trace content but cannot fully compensate for the information loss in the truncated majority.\nThe 84.45\\% accuracy reflects a combination of trace information loss and model capability constraints.\n\n\\subsection{Error Analysis}\n\\label{sec:error_analysis}\n\nSeed robustness testing revealed that the two errors made by \\texttt{exception\\_only} (accuracy = 99.83\\%, 2/1190 incorrect) are \\emph{identical across all three seeds} (42, 123, 456).\nBoth errors are false negatives on samples involving the \\texttt{check\\_dict\\_case} function (sample IDs 901 and 905): the ground truth is \\texttt{pass}, but the model predicts \\texttt{fail}.\nThis 100\\% error overlap ($\\sigma{=}0$) across seeds demonstrates that these are not stochastic training artifacts but \\emph{irreducible samples} at the boundary of what exception-based trace signals can resolve.\n\nManual inspection reveals the root cause: \\texttt{check\\_dict\\_case} (HumanEval/95) uses \\texttt{all()} over a generator expression, and Python's \\texttt{all()} short-circuits on the first \\texttt{True} value, raising \\texttt{GeneratorExit} to terminate the generator.\nBecause \\texttt{GeneratorExit} is a \\texttt{BaseException} subclass, the tracer records it as seven \\texttt{[ERROR] GeneratorExit} lines---even though the function executes correctly and the test passes.\nThe model observes these spurious error markers and reasonably but incorrectly predicts \\texttt{fail}.\nThis is not a model failure but a \\emph{representation ceiling}: the \\texttt{exception\\_only} format cannot distinguish genuine exceptions from benign \\texttt{BaseException} subclasses triggered by Python's iterator protocol.\nThe 99.83\\% accuracy is therefore a deterministic upper bound for this trace representation on this dataset, not an indication of insufficient model capacity.\n\n\\subsection{Limitations}\n\nOur study has several limitations.\nFirst, all experiments use a single dataset (591 problems from HumanEval and MBPP-sanitized); generalization to other languages, larger codebases, or different fault types remains untested.\nSecond, we evaluate only two models at the 7B parameter scale; the relationship between model size and trace signal utility is unexplored.\nThird, DeepSeek \\texttt{exception\\_only} exhibits seed-dependent variance: 2/3 seeds achieve 99.33\\% while seed 123 yields 91.09\\% ($\\sigma{=}4.76$pp, $n{=}3$), attributable to LoRA initialization sensitivity (fail recall = 70.5\\% for the outlier vs.\\ 100\\% for others).\nQwen shows perfect seed robustness ($\\sigma{=}0$, $n{=}3$).\nWhether training stabilization techniques (e.g., larger warmup ratio, LoRA rank adjustment) can eliminate DeepSeek's seed sensitivity is unexplored.\nOther conditions use a single seed.\nFourth, the DeepSeek \\texttt{full\\_trace} condition was invalidated because the DeepSeek tokenizer produced sequences exceeding 2,048 tokens, causing the verdict label to be truncated during training; the model consequently failed to learn the classification task, and we report it as N/A rather than presenting potentially misleading results.\nFifth, the candidate solutions were generated by Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct, which also serves as one of our evaluation models.\nThis does not affect verdict prediction---the task is to classify a \\emph{given} solution as pass or fail, not to generate solutions---but we note the overlap for transparency.\nMoreover, the pass/fail distribution in the dataset is determined by the generating model's coding ability; a stronger or weaker generator would yield a different class balance, potentially affecting task difficulty and the generalizability of our accuracy estimates.\nFinally, our analysis is limited to verdict prediction; whether exception dominance extends to other classification tasks (vulnerability detection, clone detection) or to generation tasks (program repair) is an open question.",
